# Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges: Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places

Delip Rao ^(†)^(†)thanks: Corresponding author. Affiliation: University of Pennsylvania Email: [delip@upenn.edu](mailto:)    Chris Callison-Burch Affiliation: University of Pennsylvania Email: [ccb@upenn.edu](mailto:)

###### Abstract

LLM judges score outputs against rubrics well enough to have become the norm, both in benchmarks and as rewards for training. Jev, a classifier-like alternative its creators call a “decision model”, returns probabilities over permitted answers with a calibrated confidence score, which LLM judges do not natively provide. We compare Jev with three flash-tier LLM judges on nine panels from seven benchmarks with human judgments, giving every judge identical criterion texts. The LLM judges run in two setups: holistically, reading a whole rubric at once as Jev does, and one criterion at a time. Jev can often stand in for them. They cost 16 to 325 times as much and take 28 to 350 times as long, yet in each setup Jev’s accuracy differs significantly from theirs in at most 8 of 27 paired comparisons, ahead mostly on binary checklist criteria and behind only on ordinal ones. Despite their different designs, the two kinds of judge err alike. On ordinal criteria, all LLM judges and Jev depart from the human raters together, agreeing more with one another than with the labels and mostly assigning lower levels. On Jev’s most confident errors, about 96% of LLM verdicts repeat its wrong answer, where independent errors would give about half. Intuitively, calibrated confidence should make Jev an ideal first stage of a cascade that defers uncertain verdicts to an LLM judge. Yet such cascades only lower cost while adding little accuracy: even with oracle thresholds, none beats the best single judge by more than 2.7 points. Calibration can tell a cascade when to defer, but the cascade also needs a fallback that errs elsewhere; these judges are wrong in the same places. These findings, which hold in both setups and at high reasoning effort, suggest that a cascade of judges succeeds only when its judges make complementary errors, and that future decision models should be designed afresh with that aim.

Figure 1: In most comparisons, LLM-based rubric judges are not separated (Section [3.4](#S3.SS4 "3.4 Metrics and statistics ‣ 3 Judges, Panels, and Protocol ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")) from Jev (Section [3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Jev ‣ 3 Judges, Panels, and Protocol ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")) in accuracy, though they cost far more. Similar results are observed with holistic rubric judges (Figure [N1](#A14.F1 "Figure N1 ‣ Accuracy by judging mode. ‣ Appendix N Whole-Rubric Judgments ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")).

## 1 Introduction

LLM judges now return verdicts on the individual criteria of rubrics, for benchmarks ([Arora et al., 2025](#bib.bib2)) and as training rewards ([Gunjal et al., 2026](#bib.bib23)), and are validated against human labels ([Zheng et al., 2023](#bib.bib11); [Bavaresco et al., 2025](#bib.bib16)). We call the judged submission a *unit* and a (unit, criterion) combination a *pair*. A per-criterion grader sends each pair as its own request ([Rao and Callison-Burch, 2026](#bib.bib24)), so cost grows with units times criteria.

A *typed classifier* answers a question about a structured input with a probability distribution over a fixed set of permitted answers and writes no free text. Jev, from TypeSafe, is one; its creators call it a ‘‘System One’’ model¹¹ 1 The name comes from the System 1/System 2 distinction in [Kahneman (2011)](#bib.bib50) between fast, automatic judgment and slow, deliberate reasoning. Vendor documentation: [https://docs.typesafe.ai](https://docs.typesafe.ai), accessed 23 September 2026. and a “decision model”. It judges every criterion of a unit in one request, with a probability or confidence for each answer, so it could replace an LLM judge or pick the pairs that need one. It returns only probabilities while LLM judges reason before they answer, a difference in design that suggests the two would err in different places, which a cascade needs in order to beat the LLM judge it defers to.

|--------------|------------------|----------|------------|-------|
| Panel        | Unit             | Criteria | Scale      | Pairs |
| RiceChem     | chemistry answer | 27       | binary     | 819   |
| HealthBench  | chatbot reply    | 34       | binary     | 406   |
| ELLIPSE      | learner essay    | 6        | 5 levels   | 1,548 |
| FED-Turn     | dialogue turn    | 7 + 1    | 3 levels   | 600   |
| FED-Dialogue | dialogue         | 9 + 1    | 3 levels   | 250   |
| HelpSteer2   | assistant reply  | 4        | 5 levels   | 360   |
| LFQA         | ELI5 answer      | 3        | 3–4 levels | 360   |
| USR-TC       | dialogue reply   | 3 + 2    | 3 levels   | 360   |
| USR-PC       | dialogue reply   | 3 + 2    | 3 levels   | 300   |

Sources: [Sonkar et al. (2024)](#bib.bib1), [Arora et al. (2025)](#bib.bib2), [Crossley et al. (2023)](#bib.bib3), [Mehri and Eskenazi (2020a)](#bib.bib4) (FED), [Wang et al. (2024b)](#bib.bib7), [Kamoda et al. (2025)](#bib.bib6), and [Mehri and Eskenazi (2020b)](#bib.bib5). Details: Table [A1](#A1.T1 "Table A1 ‣ Appendix A Panels, Judges, and Protocol ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places").

We set Jev against three *flash-tier* LLM judges, from tiers their vendors call fast and lower-priced (Appendix [A](#A1 "Appendix A Panels, Judges, and Protocol ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")): GPT-5.6 Luna, Gemini 3.8 Flash, and DeepSeek V4.1 Flash (Luna, Gemini, and DeepSeek); earlier work also chose smaller judges partly to cut cost ([Verga et al., 2024](#bib.bib17); [Chlapanis et al., 2025](#bib.bib48); [Xie et al., 2026](#bib.bib49)). All four judges receive the same 5,003 pairs from nine *panels*, sampled from seven public benchmarks with human judgments (Table [1](#S1.T1 "Table 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")): two *binary panels* with only binary criteria and seven *ordinal panels* that rate most criteria on ordered levels. Every judge reads the same criterion texts, fixed in advance with no examples, rewording, or tuning. We run the LLM judges one criterion at a time, on whole rubrics as Jev reads them, and at high reasoning effort, and ask three questions whose answers hold in all three conditions (Table [3](#S4.T3 "Table 3 ‣ 4.3 Judging mode and reasoning effort ‣ 4 Jev Can Often Replace an LLM Judge ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")):

1.  RQ1.
    Can a typed classifier stand in for LLM judges, and at what cost and speed?
2.  RQ2.
    How different are the classifier’s verdicts from the LLM judges’?
3.  RQ3.
    How well does a confidence-based cascade from the classifier to an LLM judge work?

RQ1: Often, and far more cheaply. Against per-criterion LLM judges, Jev differs significantly in only 8 of 27 paired accuracy comparisons, leading mostly on binary panels and trailing only on ordinal ones, and reaches parity (within 5 points) in 8 more (Figure [1](#S0.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places"); Section [4](#S4 "4 Jev Can Often Replace an LLM Judge ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). Across both judging modes, the LLM judges’ costs were 16 to 325 times Jev’s and their wall times 28 to 350 times.

RQ2: Less than their designs suggest. On each ordinal panel the judges agree with one another more than with the labels, and on six of the seven, Jev and every LLM judge place units lower on average than the raters did, a pattern unstated rating conventions could produce (Section [5](#S5 "5 Jev and the LLM Judges Err Alike ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). They make *correlated errors*, the “same places” of our title: when one judge is wrong, another gives the same wrong verdict more often than independent errors would. On Jev’s most confident errors, 96.0% of LLM verdicts repeat its answer, against 50.3% under independence.

RQ3: Well for cost, not for accuracy. Jev’s probabilities are calibrated on the binary panels and its confidence ranks its errors on six ordinal panels, as a first stage requires. But a cascade gains over its fallback only on kept pairs that Jev alone gets right, and correlated errors leave few. Cascades cut cost, yet even with oracle thresholds they beat the best single judge by at most 2.7 points (Section [6](#S6 "6 Correlated Errors Undo the Cascade Advantage ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")).

## 2 Related Work

LLM judges show position, verbosity, and self-enhancement biases, yet GPT-4 agrees with human preferences about as often as humans do ([Zheng et al., 2023](#bib.bib11)), and LLMs given the evaluators’ instructions rate consistently with experts ([Chiang and Lee, 2023](#bib.bib13)); on JUDGE-BENCH, which includes both USR sets and scores ordinal ratings by correlation, reliability varies with property and annotator expertise ([Bavaresco et al., 2025](#bib.bib16)). High agreement can hide score differences ([Thakur et al., 2025](#bib.bib19)), uncertain labels can make a machine look human-level ([Elangovan et al., 2025](#bib.bib21)), and LLM essay graders score below humans ([Kundu and Barbosa, 2024](#bib.bib34)) and penalize minor errors that humans discount ([Mathew et al., 2026](#bib.bib35)), so we report the offset.

Prometheus and Prometheus 2 train open evaluators to score against user-supplied rubrics ([Kim et al., 2024a](#bib.bib14); [Kim et al., 2024b](#bib.bib15)), CheckEval’s binary checklists raise agreement ([Lee et al., 2025](#bib.bib25)), HealthBench refined its criteria for its grader ([Arora et al., 2025](#bib.bib2)), and LLM-Rubric maps answer distributions to each human’s labels ([Hashemi et al., 2024](#bib.bib22)), a layer that would absorb an offset; we fit no such layer.

Across more than 350 models, larger and more accurate models make highly correlated errors, even across providers ([Kim et al., 2025](#bib.bib31)), mistakes grow more alike with capability ([Goel et al., 2025](#bib.bib32)), and juries of smaller judges from different families can reduce bias ([Verga et al., 2024](#bib.bib17)). Closed-set probabilities carry information about correctness ([Guo et al., 2017](#bib.bib29); [Kadavath et al., 2022](#bib.bib30)) and serve to abstain ([Geifman and El-Yaniv, 2017](#bib.bib28)) or to escalate to costlier models ([Chen et al., 2024](#bib.bib27)); Trust or Escalate sets its escalation threshold on a calibration set to guarantee human agreement ([Jung et al., 2025](#bib.bib26)), whereas we replay cascades with oracle and cross-fitted thresholds (Section [6](#S6 "6 Correlated Errors Undo the Cascade Advantage ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")).

## 3 Judges, Panels, and Protocol

All judges run in AutoRubric’s open-source criterion grader ([Rao and Callison-Burch, 2026](#bib.bib24)), the *harness*. An ordinal criterion’s options are ordered levels, and its pairs are *ordinal pairs*.

### 3.1 Jev

Jev costs \$0.042 per million input tokens; output is free. A request carries one question per criterion and a record (the vendor’s “state”) holding the unit’s input and submission, as an LLM judge receives them. Jev never sees the harness’s system prompt and sees options in scale order.

Jev has three *primitives*, each with a *decoding rule*: Noul returns the probability of yes, thresholded at 0.5; Choice a distribution over option labels, read by argmax; and Score the expected level over ordered level descriptions, rounded to the closest level, ties to even (Appendix [E](#A5 "Appendix E Choice Against Score ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). A *framing* is a primitive with our question wording (Table [A2](#A1.T2 "Table A2 ‣ Appendix A Panels, Judges, and Protocol ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")).

On the binary panels *Jev Choice* asks Choice over MET, UNMET, and CANNOT_ASSESS, and *Jev Noul* asks Noul, both through the *wrapper*, a fixed instruction with the harness’s MET and UNMET definitions. On the ordinal panels Jev Choice gets the criterion sentence as instruction and the LLM judges’ option texts, the *NA option* (“Cannot assess / not applicable”) included. *Jev Score* reads the same texts as level descriptions without that option, so it cannot abstain; both use Jev Noul on the 364 *embedded binary pairs* of the FED and USR panels. Jev Choice and the LLM judges are the *matched judges*, and with Jev Score the *five judges*. Jev’s *confidence*, used only to rank pairs, is the confidence that Choice and Score return, or $2\hspace{0pt}{|{{P{(\text{yes})}} - 0.5}|}$ for Noul (Appendix [I](#A9 "Appendix I Confidence ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")).

### 3.2 The LLM judges

Each LLM judge runs through the harness with its default system prompts, DeepSeek via OpenRouter (Appendix [A](#A1 "Appendix A Panels, Judges, and Protocol ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")), options shuffled per pair by a logged seed against position bias ([Wang et al., 2024a](#bib.bib18)), temperature 0 except for Luna’s enforced 1.0, and undated model identifiers (Table [A2](#A1.T2 "Table A2 ‣ Appendix A Panels, Judges, and Protocol ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). It returns a verdict and an explanation, not a probability. The *main runs* (19 September 2026) set no reasoning effort, so vendor defaults applied: medium for Luna and Gemini, high on DeepSeek’s own API, and undocumented on OpenRouter (Appendix [O](#A15 "Appendix O Reasoning Effort ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")).

There are two *judging modes*. With *per-criterion judgments*, the harness default and the mode of our main runs, the judge takes one criterion of a unit at a time; with *whole-rubric (holistic) judgments* it reads the unit’s whole rubric at once and still returns one verdict per criterion. Both practices exist ([Liu et al., 2023](#bib.bib12); [Lin and Chen, 2023](#bib.bib38)). Whole-rubric judgments make the LLM judges comparable with Jev; per-criterion judgments stay primary, following work that recommends judging criteria separately to avoid the halo effects documented in human rating ([Thorndike, 1920](#bib.bib43); [Cooper, 1981](#bib.bib44)) and related effects in LLM judges ([Stureborg et al., 2024](#bib.bib46); [Hu et al., 2024](#bib.bib45); [Feuer et al., 2025](#bib.bib47)).

The whole-rubric runs (27 September, eight days later) kept the option orders and sent 8 requests at a time, like Jev; *repeat runs* resent the whole-rubric requests for a seeded 10% of units (106) as a run-to-run baseline (Appendix [N](#A14 "Appendix N Whole-Rubric Judgments ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). The same day, *effort runs* repeated the per-criterion judgments at high reasoning effort on all nine panels, and DeepSeek’s also at medium. An effort calibration on the repeat units supports reading Luna’s and Gemini’s main runs as medium; with DeepSeek’s medium run they form the *medium condition*, and the high runs form the *high condition* (Appendix [O](#A15 "Appendix O Reasoning Effort ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")).

### 3.3 Panels and labels

Each ordinal panel is a 10% or 20% sample of units, stratified where possible and drawn with a fixed seed before any judging (Table [A1](#A1.T1 "Table A1 ‣ Appendix A Panels, Judges, and Protocol ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). The binary panels’ criteria are checklists: question-specific presence checks on RiceChem, consensus behavior checks on HealthBench. The judges read our *rubric versions* of the benchmarks, written for a separate, unpublished study and released (Appendix [M](#A13 "Appendix M Release and Derived Statistics ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")); the raters had each benchmark’s own instructions. No judge sees the labels, the benchmarks’ overall scores, or the strata.

An ordinal pair’s *human label* is its raters’ mean level rounded to the nearest level, halves up (46.8% of ELLIPSE labels are half-way), and a binary pair’s the rater majority, or RiceChem’s teaching-assistant label. *Unanimous pairs*, whose raters all agree, and *split pairs* are reported apart, as rater disagreement can be signal ([Plank, 2022](#bib.bib20)). The *rater reference* scores each rater against the rounded mean of the others on the six ordinal panels with several raters; its target averages one rating fewer than the label, so it does not bound a judge. Two *no-read baselines* ignore the unit: HelpSteer2’s constant predictor (60.0% exact accuracy) and LFQA’s provenance baseline, which sees only whether ChatGPT or a Reddit user answered (68.1%).

Bold: best judge in its column. †: separated from Jev Choice (embedded binary pairs included); ‡: also after correction for the 27 comparisons; §: Jev Score separated from Jev Choice. Gemini’s USR-TC offset is $- 0.005$. Each judge’s $\kappa$ and Jev Noul: Table [D1](#A4.T1 "Table D1 ‣ Appendix D The Binary Panels ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places"); each judge’s mean QWK and Jev Score’s offset: Table [C3](#A3.T3 "Table C3 ‣ Appendix C Paired Comparisons and Secondary Metrics ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places"); costs: Table [B1](#A2.T1 "Table B1 ‣ Appendix B Cost and Wall Time ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places").

*Exact accuracy* is the share of ordinal pairs predicted at the label’s level, and *mean QWK* averages per-criterion quadratic-weighted kappas ([Cohen, 1968](#bib.bib40)). A judge’s *offset* is its mean predicted minus labeled level, and its *gap* its exact accuracy minus the rater reference’s. Binary criteria get *verdict accuracy*, Cohen’s $\kappa$ ([Cohen, 1960](#bib.bib39)), and the *MET-rate offset* in points. As chance-level exact accuracy varies with the number of levels, panels are compared by mean QWK, offset, or gap.

Paired comparisons use the pairs both judges scored, resampling units 10,000 times for percentile intervals. Two judges are *separated*, or differ significantly, when the 95% interval of their accuracy difference excludes zero, at *parity* when the 90% interval lies within 5 points of zero (a post-hoc margin; Table [C1](#A3.T1 "Table C1 ‣ Appendix C Paired Comparisons and Secondary Metrics ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places") gives the smallest each comparison supports), and otherwise *inconclusive*. Bootstrap $p$-values (twice the smaller share of replicates on either side of zero) are Holm-corrected within each family of comparisons, such as Jev Choice’s 27 with the LLM judges; sign tests are in Appendix [C](#A3 "Appendix C Paired Comparisons and Secondary Metrics ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places").

Abstentions and *judge-call failures*, pairs left without a usable verdict, leave the denominators, so pair sets differ between tables (Appendix [C](#A3 "Appendix C Paired Comparisons and Secondary Metrics ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). Cost is list price, DeepSeek’s its OpenRouter bill (Appendix [B](#A2 "Appendix B Cost and Wall Time ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). Level shifts, cascades, juries, and other counterfactuals are *post-hoc analyses* of the recorded verdicts, with no judge rerun.

## 4 Jev Can Often Replace an LLM Judge

### 4.1 Accuracy in paired comparisons

Jev Choice ranks first on RiceChem and second on HealthBench, behind Gemini (Table [2](#S3.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 3.4 Metrics and statistics ‣ 3 Judges, Panels, and Protocol ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")), separated from Luna on both and from Gemini on RiceChem, and at parity with DeepSeek on both (Table [C1](#A3.T1 "Table C1 ‣ Appendix C Paired Comparisons and Secondary Metrics ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). On the ordinal panels 16 of 21 comparisons are not separated; of the other five, Gemini leads on ELLIPSE, FED-Dialogue, and HelpSteer2, most on ELLIPSE (16.4 points), and Luna leads on USR-PC and trails on HelpSteer2. DeepSeek is never separated. Six of 21 reach parity; the other 10 have 90% intervals reaching 5.3 to 8.3 points from zero.

Over all 27 comparisons, 8 are separated, 8 at parity, and 11 inconclusive (Table [3](#S4.T3 "Table 3 ‣ 4.3 Judging mode and reasoning effort ‣ 4 Jev Can Often Replace an LLM Judge ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")), three of Jev Choice’s four leads falling on binary panels and all four deficits on ordinal ones. Four separations remain significant after correction, and resampling the prompts, questions, or contexts units share leaves eight (Appendix [C](#A3 "Appendix C Paired Comparisons and Secondary Metrics ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")).

On no ordinal panel is Jev Choice the most accurate matched judge; Gemini is, or ties, everywhere but LFQA. Jev Score is separated ahead of Jev Choice on four ordinal panels, never behind, and is the most accurate judge on FED-Turn and USR-TC (Appendix [E](#A5 "Appendix E Choice Against Score ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). HelpSteer2’s constant predictor beats every judge, significantly in pair-level sign tests for all but Gemini ($p = 0.075$), so separations there rank judges that trail a constant; on LFQA no judge differs significantly from the provenance baseline in exact accuracy (Appendix [H](#A8 "Appendix H Where the Conventions Live ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")).

### 4.2 Jev is cheaper and faster than every LLM judge

Over the nine panels Jev Choice cost \$0.063 (Table [B1](#A2.T1 "Table B1 ‣ Appendix B Cost and Wall Time ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")), Luna 29 times as much, Gemini 325 times, and DeepSeek 66 times; Jev Choice took 29 seconds, Luna 859, Gemini 950, and DeepSeek 6,298 (30 to 220 times as long). Each Jev framing sent 8 requests at a time and each LLM judge 16 to 32 on the ordinal panels, so these ratios understate Jev’s advantage at equal concurrency. At Jev’s concurrency, whole-rubric Luna, Gemini, and DeepSeek cost 16, 182, and 54 times as much and took 28, 45, and 350 times as long (Table [N1](#A14.T1 "Table N1 ‣ Repeat runs. ‣ Appendix N Whole-Rubric Judgments ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). At high effort, with completion tokens up 41% to 180% over the medium condition, the LLM judges cost 36 to 675 times as much as Jev Choice (Table [O1](#A15.T1 "Table O1 ‣ Requests. ‣ Appendix O Reasoning Effort ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")).

### 4.3 Judging mode and reasoning effort

|---------------------------|----------------------|---------------------|-------------------|

Table 3: Answers by LLM-judge condition (Jev’s verdicts fixed). Triples: Luna/Gemini/DeepSeek, multiples of Jev Choice’s cost and wall time (concurrency: Section [4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Jev is cheaper and faster than every LLM judge ‣ 4 Jev Can Often Replace an LLM Judge ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")); DeepSeek’s high wall time is unmeasured. Leads on binary panels in parentheses. RQ2 (Section [5](#S5 "5 Jev and the LLM Judges Err Alike ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")): ordinal panels with judge–judge above judge–label QWK and with every matched offset negative; LLM verdicts (%) repeating Jev’s confident errors (baseline). RQ3 (Section [6](#S6 "6 Correlated Errors Undo the Cascade Advantage ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")): largest gain over the best single judge (points); panels where a cross-fitted cascade matching the best LLM judge costs under half of it.

With whole-rubric judges (Table [3](#S4.T3 "Table 3 ‣ 4.3 Judging mode and reasoning effort ‣ 4 Jev Can Often Replace an LLM Judge ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")), two of Jev Choice’s three separated deficits are against Luna (ELLIPSE and FED-Turn), and correction within this mode’s 27 comparisons leaves only Luna’s and Gemini’s ELLIPSE leads significant (Figure [N1](#A14.F1 "Figure N1 ‣ Accuracy by judging mode. ‣ Appendix N Whole-Rubric Judgments ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places"); Table [N3](#A14.T3 "Table N3 ‣ Accuracy by judging mode. ‣ Appendix N Whole-Rubric Judgments ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). The mode raised Luna’s and Gemini’s ELLIPSE accuracy by 5.9 and 6.1 points, the only two of the 27 per-judge changes still significant once corrected, while their offsets there moved toward zero; without ELLIPSE their pooled changes are negative but not separated (Appendix [N](#A14 "Appendix N Whole-Rubric Judgments ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). Switching modes changed more verdicts than an identical repeat did (Table [N4](#A14.T4 "Table N4 ‣ Jev Choice against the whole-rubric judges. ‣ Appendix N Whole-Rubric Judgments ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")), though the runs were eight days apart (see [Limitations](#Sx1 "Limitations ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")).

At high effort each judge’s pooled accuracy moves by at most 0.5 points; of the 27 per-panel changes, only Gemini’s ELLIPSE gain of 2.5 points stays significant under the correction, and Jev Choice keeps five of eight separations, none reversed (Table [O3](#A15.T3 "Table O3 ‣ Calibration. ‣ Appendix O Reasoning Effort ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). DeepSeek’s medium run adds two separations in opposite directions (Jev Choice ahead on RiceChem by 3.3 points, DeepSeek on USR-TC by 4.7), so the medium condition separates 10 of 27 comparisons; neither is separated at high effort (Appendix [O](#A15 "Appendix O Reasoning Effort ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")).

## 5 Jev and the LLM Judges Err Alike

### 5.1 A shared shortfall on ordinal criteria

Figure 2: (a) Share of ELLIPSE pairs at each level: labels (gray bars) and the five judges (DeepSeek over its 1,543 answered pairs). (b) USR engaging: exact accuracy before (marker) and after (arrowhead) a post-hoc +1 shift; dashed: each corpus’s rater reference on engaging (Table [G4](#A7.T4 "Table G4 ‣ Traits. ‣ Appendix G The Offset in Detail ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")).

On all seven ordinal panels, agreement among the judges exceeds their agreement with the labels, on five even the least-agreeing pair beating the best judge–label agreement (Tables [2](#S3.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 3.4 Metrics and statistics ‣ 3 Judges, Panels, and Protocol ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")b and [N5](#A14.T5 "Table N5 ‣ Agreement between runs. ‣ Appendix N Whole-Rubric Judgments ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")b), a pattern that label noise or a common offset could produce. Every judge falls short of the rater reference on ELLIPSE, FED-Dialogue, and USR-PC but exceeds it on LFQA, and the matched judges’ mean QWK with the labels trails the reference’s only on ELLIPSE and USR-PC. On ELLIPSE the best, Gemini, reaches 29.8% exact accuracy against 51.4%, and all four matched judges are wrong on 64.0% of pairs, so choosing a correct judge for each pair would reach only 36.0% (Table [J2](#A10.T2 "Table J2 ‣ Appendix J Repeated Errors and Juries ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")).

Every matched judge’s offset is negative on the six ordinal panels other than USR-TC, as are 31 of the five judges’ 35 offsets (Tables [2](#S3.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 3.4 Metrics and statistics ‣ 3 Judges, Panels, and Protocol ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")c and [C3](#A3.T3 "Table C3 ‣ Appendix C Paired Comparisons and Secondary Metrics ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")h). ELLIPSE’s are largest: its labels put 36.3% of pairs at levels 4–5, and no judge more than 4.2% (Figure [2](#S5.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ 5.1 A shared shortfall on ordinal criteria ‣ 5 Jev and the LLM Judges Err Alike ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")a).

A judge’s *location* is where on the scale it places a criterion’s units. Over the 31 criteria with a rater reference, the matched judges’ mean offset tracks their mean gap (Spearman $\rho = 0.92$; Figure [G1](#A7.F1 "Figure G1 ‣ Criteria far below the reference. ‣ Appendix G The Offset in Detail ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")), in part by construction, since an offset costs exact accuracy. A leave-one-out shift of each criterion’s predictions (Appendix [G](#A7 "Appendix G The Offset in Detail ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")) removes the relation ($\rho = 0.16$) and leaves no criterion more than 8.4 points below the unshifted reference, a comparison favoring the judges; with the raters shifted too, four criteria stay more than 5 points short (Table [G2](#A7.T2 "Table G2 ‣ Leave-one-out and best shifts. ‣ Appendix G The Offset in Detail ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). The offset leaves the essays’ order largely intact ($\rho = 0.62$ to $0.72$ with ELLIPSE’s holistic score; Table [C3](#A3.T3 "Table C3 ‣ Appendix C Paired Comparisons and Secondary Metrics ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")d), and moving every ELLIPSE prediction up a level lifts Jev Choice from 13.4% to 50.4%, though the raters need no shift (Figure [G2](#A7.F2 "Figure G2 ‣ Traits. ‣ Appendix G The Offset in Detail ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")).

### 5.2 Conventions the criterion texts leave unstated

*Conventions* are rating rules that the raters followed but our texts omit. Table [H1](#A8.T1 "Table H1 ‣ Appendix H Where the Conventions Live ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places") lists candidates of three kinds (a population norm, a corpus-relative standard, and annotation habits), found by reading the judges’ errors and so exploratory; the raters’ instructions may state some (we checked ELLIPSE only). On fixed pairs the offsets share their sign across the three LLM judges in both judging modes (Table [N5](#A14.T5 "Table N5 ‣ Agreement between runs. ‣ Appendix N Whole-Rubric Judgments ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")) and across Jev Choice, which never sees the harness prompt, and Jev Score, which decodes by expected level, so neither a shared prompt nor a decoding rule is their common cause. USR engaging, byte-identical on Topical-Chat and PersonaChat, draws matched-judge offsets of $- 0.73$ to $- 1.03$ levels on PersonaChat, whose raters gave the top level in 59.4% of ratings, and $- 0.01$ to $- 0.31$ on Topical-Chat, where they gave it in 34.7%, with similar orderings on both ($\rho = 0.50$ to $0.72$ with the rater mean). A one-level shift lifts every matched judge on PersonaChat from at most 26.7% to at least 69.5% and lowers it on Topical-Chat (Figure [2](#S5.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ 5.1 A shared shortfall on ordinal criteria ‣ 5 Jev and the LLM Judges Err Alike ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")b), partly mechanically (63.3% of PersonaChat labels are at the top); we read these labels as corpus-relative (Appendix [G](#A7 "Appendix G The Offset in Detail ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")).

ELLIPSE’s labels may carry a population norm: its trained raters scored the essays of grade 8 to 12 English learners ([Crossley et al., 2023](#bib.bib3)), a norm-referenced scale reads a level against a population ([Glaser, 1963](#bib.bib41)), and training instills such expectations ([Weigle, 1994](#bib.bib42)). Our level-5 label, “native-like facility”, and LLM essay graders’ penalty for small errors ([Mathew et al., 2026](#bib.bib35)) may also contribute (Appendix [H](#A8 "Appendix H Where the Conventions Live ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")).

Label noise would scatter errors on both sides of the label and gather them on split pairs, so it cannot explain ELLIPSE, where 99.5% of Jev Choice’s errors lie below the label, LFQA factuality, where it misses unanimous pairs more often than split ones, or USR-TC’s almost unanimous \_nofact pairs, whose context holds no fact; on other panels the evidence is weaker (Appendix [H](#A8 "Appendix H Where the Conventions Live ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). A shared reluctance to grant top levels and shared training priors ([Kim et al., 2025](#bib.bib31)) remain possible, and no experiment varied the text, so the account is observational.

### 5.3 The LLM judges repeat Jev’s confident errors

An LLM verdict that repeats Jev Choice’s wrong answer is a *repeated error*, and the share of repeated errors on Jev’s wrong pairs measures correlated errors. Since judges sharing an offset would often agree anyway, the *independence baseline* is each judge’s rate of giving Jev’s wrong answer on other pairs with the same criterion and label. Over all of Jev Choice’s errors the share beats the baseline on every ordinal panel, by 18.8 to 34.2 points on six and 8.6 on ELLIPSE, where the judges’ low placement explains most repeats (Table [I1](#A9.T1 "Table I1 ‣ The returned field. ‣ Appendix I Confidence ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")c). The *confident-error sample* holds Jev Choice’s 12 most confident errors per ordinal panel; 242 of the 252 LLM verdicts on these 84 pairs (96.0%) repeat its answer, against 50.3% expected, and slightly more in the other conditions (Table [3](#S4.T3 "Table 3 ‣ 4.3 Judging mode and reasoning effort ‣ 4 Jev Can Often Replace an LLM Judge ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")); HealthBench shows the same (Appendix [J](#A10 "Appendix J Repeated Errors and Juries ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). Rater disagreement may explain repeats on split pairs, which hold 62 of the 72 confident errors on multi-rater panels, but not on unanimous pairs, such as a \_nofact pair.

## 6 Correlated Errors Undo the Cascade Advantage

RQ3 asks how well a confidence-based cascade works. A *cascade* lets a cheap judge answer every pair and sends the *deferred pairs*, whose confidence falls below a threshold, to a *fallback judge*; the cheap judge’s verdicts stand on the *kept pairs*. An *oracle threshold* is chosen on the pairs it is evaluated on, so its gains are optimistic; a *cross-fitted threshold* is chosen on a random half of the units and evaluated on the other. A cheap judge whose confidence tracks its errors is the natural first stage, but it lifts the cascade above the fallback only on kept pairs where it alone is right, and Section [5.3](#S5.SS3 "5.3 The LLM judges repeat Jev’s confident errors ‣ 5 Jev and the LLM Judges Err Alike ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places") suggests these are few.

### 6.1 Jev’s confidence ranks its own errors

Figure 3: Jev Choice’s confidence band against (a) its error rate and (b) the share of LLM verdicts on its wrong pairs that repeat its answer (solid; dashed: independence baseline), one line per ordinal panel, ELLIPSE in black. Bands with under 20 pairs (a) or errors (b) are omitted.

Jev Choice’s errors gather among its less confident verdicts on every ordinal panel but ELLIPSE (Figure [3](#S6.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 6.1 Jev’s confidence ranks its own errors ‣ 6 Correlated Errors Undo the Cascade Advantage ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")a); on those six, the area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) of its confidence for correctness is 0.57 to 0.70. On ELLIPSE it is 0.49, its error rate flat across bands, and confidence fails on some criteria, such as LFQA factuality (0.41; Appendix [I](#A9 "Appendix I Confidence ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). On the binary panels, the only ones where we measure calibration, P(MET) separates MET from UNMET at an AUROC of 0.80 to 0.86 with an expected calibration error of 0.056 to 0.096 (Table [D1](#A4.T1 "Table D1 ‣ Appendix D The Binary Panels ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). Yet the LLM judges’ repeats, and their excess over the baseline, peak on every panel in Jev’s top confidence band (80.5% to 92.8% against 33.6% to 63.7%; Figure [3](#S6.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 6.1 Jev’s confidence ranks its own errors ‣ 6 Correlated Errors Undo the Cascade Advantage ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")b); these are the verdicts a cascade keeps.

### 6.2 Cascades lower cost but add little accuracy

|              |      |                  |                     |                              |                |                 |
|--------------|------|------------------|---------------------|------------------------------|----------------|-----------------|
|              |      | Cascade vs. best |                     |                              | Match best LLM |                 |
| Panel        | Best | Oracle           |  Cross- fitted      |  Halv. $\phantom{} > 0$      | Cost (%)       |  Held- out      |
| RiceChem     | 81.0 | $- 0.4$          | $- 1.0$             | 6                            | 6.6            | $+ 1.2$         |
| HealthBench  | 79.6 | $+ 2.0$          | $+ 1.5$             | 80                           | 15.6           | $- 0.5$         |
| ELLIPSE      | 29.8 | $0.0$            | $- 0.1$             | 0                            | 99.8           | $- 0.1$         |
| FED-Turn     | 70.7 | $- 5.2$          | $- 7.3$             | 0                            | 45.5           | $- 0.7$         |
| FED-Dialogue | 59.7 | $0.0$            | $- 0.9$             | 40                           | 77.9           | $- 2.1$         |
| HelpSteer2   | 54.3 | $+ 0.6$          | $- 0.9$             | 4                            | 35.6           | $- 1.8$         |
| LFQA         | 71.5 | $+ 0.6$          | $+ 0.6$             | 52                           | 39.0           | $- 0.7$         |
| USR-TC       | 70.6 | $+ 0.3$          | $- 0.7$             | 30                           | 47.5           | $- 1.1$         |
| USR-PC       | 74.6 | $+ 1.3$          | $+ 0.8$             | 76                           | 31.3           | $- 0.2$         |

Table 4: Post-hoc Jev-first cascades. Best: accuracy (%) of the best single judge (Jev Choice on RiceChem, Jev Score on FED-Turn, Luna on LFQA, Gemini elsewhere). Oracle, cross-fitted: cascade minus best (points); halv. $\phantom{} > 0$: halvings (%) with a gain. Match best LLM: cost share (Jev’s run included) and held-out difference (points) of the cross-fitted cascade matching DeepSeek (RiceChem), Luna (LFQA), or Gemini. Pair set: Appendix [C](#A3 "Appendix C Paired Comparisons and Secondary Metrics ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places"); more: Appendix [K](#A11 "Appendix K Cascades ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places").

We replay Jev-first cascades, Jev Choice first on the ordinal panels and Jev Noul on the binary ones (Appendix [K](#A11 "Appendix K Cascades ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")), against the *best single judge*, chosen from all judges including Jev’s framings; cross-fitted thresholds use 50 halvings, each half choosing its own fallback and best single judge.

With cross-fitted thresholds no cascade’s average gain over the best single judge exceeds 1.5 points (HealthBench; Table [4](#S6.T4 "Table 4 ‣ 6.2 Cascades lower cost but add little accuracy ‣ 6 Correlated Errors Undo the Cascade Advantage ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")); cascades trail it on six of nine panels, by 0.1 to 7.3 points. Oracle thresholds lift the largest gain to 2.0 points (HealthBench), but neither it nor the next, 1.3 (USR-PC), is significant in a kept-pair sign test (Table [K2](#A11.T2 "Table K2 ‣ Appendix K Cascades ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")).

A cascade and its fallback agree on the deferred pairs, so

|     |                                                                                                                                                                    |     |     |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|-----|
|     | $${{a_{\text{cascade}} - a_{\text{fallback}}} = {s_{\text{kept}}\hspace{0pt}\left( {a_{\text{Jev}}^{\text{kept}} - a_{\text{fallback}}^{\text{kept}}} \right)}},$$ |     | (1) |

with $a$ denoting accuracy and $s_{\text{kept}}$ the kept share. Against the best cascade’s fallback, Jev alone is right on only 12 to 37 pairs of an ordinal panel (Table [C2](#A3.T2 "Table C2 ‣ Appendix C Paired Comparisons and Secondary Metrics ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")), and on kept pairs the two are mostly right or wrong together (Table [K2](#A11.T2 "Table K2 ‣ Appendix K Cascades ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). The fallback’s lead on deferred pairs lifts a cascade above Jev but, by Equation ([1](#S6.E1 "In 6.2 Cascades lower cost but add little accuracy ‣ 6 Correlated Errors Undo the Cascade Advantage ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")), never above the fallback.

When the fallback is the best LLM judge, cross-fitted cascades cost 16% to 48% as much as that judge on six panels for 0.2 to 1.8 points less held-out accuracy, and 6.6% on RiceChem, where Jev alone beats it, for 1.2 points more; on ELLIPSE and FED-Dialogue even an oracle threshold must defer nearly every pair to match it (Appendix [K](#A11 "Appendix K Cascades ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")).

A whole-rubric fallback is charged a full request for every unit with a deferred pair, so those cascades save less; they gain most on HelpSteer2, where whole-rubric Gemini lost 4.5 points (Table [N5](#A14.T5 "Table N5 ‣ Agreement between runs. ‣ Appendix N Whole-Rubric Judgments ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). At high effort the gains grew most where the best single judge lost accuracy, and kept-pair sign tests give $p = 0.019$ on HealthBench, after 11 of Gemini’s kept verdicts changed, and $p = 0.035$ on LFQA, both uncorrected (Table [O4](#A15.T4 "Table O4 ‣ Accuracy. ‣ Appendix O Reasoning Effort ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places"); Appendix [O](#A15 "Appendix O Reasoning Effort ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). In no condition does a cascade’s gain over its panel’s best single judge exceed 2.7 points (oracle) or 2.5 (cross-fitted), and more reasoning did not make the LLM judges’ errors less like Jev’s.

A *jury*, which pools judges’ verdicts, fares no better. The three LLM judges’ median level (their majority on binary pairs) never beats the most accurate matched judge (chosen on the same pairs, which favors it), trailing it by up to 12.8 points (ELLIPSE), significantly so on ELLIPSE, HelpSteer2, and RiceChem (95% intervals below zero); Jev Choice as a fourth juror never helps significantly; and the median gives Jev’s wrong answer on 82 of the 84 confident errors (Table [J2](#A10.T2 "Table J2 ‣ Appendix J Repeated Errors and Juries ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places"); Appendix [J](#A10 "Appendix J Repeated Errors and Juries ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). No median jury’s interval lies above that judge in the other conditions (Tables [N5](#A14.T5 "Table N5 ‣ Agreement between runs. ‣ Appendix N Whole-Rubric Judgments ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places") and [O4](#A15.T4 "Table O4 ‣ Accuracy. ‣ Appendix O Reasoning Effort ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")).

## 7 Discussion

Prior work finds LLMs’ errors correlated across providers ([Kim et al., 2025](#bib.bib31); [Goel et al., 2025](#bib.bib32)); we find them correlated across two kinds of judge, and though we tried no larger fallback, these results give little reason to expect one to err elsewhere. TypeSafe warns that Jev may read scope and negation literally,²² 2 [https://docs.typesafe.ai/model-jaggedness/jev-1.13](https://docs.typesafe.ai/model-jaggedness/jev-1.13). yet LLM judges told to heed both (Appendix [A](#A1 "Appendix A Panels, Judges, and Protocol ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")) also placed units below the raters and repeated nearly all its confident errors, even at high effort. Literal reading by every judge, unstated conventions, shared training priors, a reluctance to grant top levels (Section [5.2](#S5.SS2 "5.2 Conventions the criterion texts leave unstated ‣ 5 Jev and the LLM Judges Err Alike ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")), and, on split pairs, rater disagreement (Section [5.3](#S5.SS3 "5.3 The LLM judges repeat Jev’s confident errors ‣ 5 Jev and the LLM Judges Err Alike ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")) could each contribute; our data cannot tell them apart.

Overlapping errors may explain why our juries of diverse judges ([Verga et al., 2024](#bib.bib17)) did not help. Before trusting a cascade, one can compare the share of repeated errors with the independence baseline (Section [5.3](#S5.SS3 "5.3 The LLM judges repeat Jev’s confident errors ‣ 5 Jev and the LLM Judges Err Alike ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")) on the labeled pairs that set its threshold ([Jung et al., 2025](#bib.bib26)), to see how far its judges err alike. Appendix [P](#A16 "Appendix P Practical Recommendations ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places") gives this and other practical recommendations, and Appendix [L](#A12 "Appendix L Binary-Panel Expectations Re-examined ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places") revisits our binary-panel expectations.

## 8 Conclusion

A cascade that puts a cheap judge ahead of an LLM fallback can beat the fallback only where the cheap judge alone is right. It therefore needs a cheap judge that knows when it is likely to be wrong and a fallback that is wrong somewhere else. Jev’s confidence meets the first need on most panels. The second went unmet: Jev returns only probabilities and the LLM judges reason before answering, yet the judges were wrong in the same places, and the LLM judges repeated almost all of Jev’s most confident errors, about twice as often as independent errors would. No cascade we replayed, even with oracle thresholds, gained more than 2.7 points over the best single judge. Jev is often a cheap substitute for a flash-tier LLM judge but a poor complement to one. A judge built differently need not be wrong differently, so the next decision model should be designed from the start to get right what LLM judges get wrong.

## Limitations

#### Observational account.

We formed our expectations about Jev on the binary panels and tested them on the ordinal panels, and our account of the judges’ shortfall came afterwards and is exploratory. No experiment supplied exemplars or rewrote level descriptions. The account could be tested by rerunning ELLIPSE with exemplar essays for each level and with a grammar scale that names observable features and drops the native-like label for level 5, and by rerunning every ordinal panel with its benchmark’s own instructions to raters in place of our rubric version. Giving the same texts to untrained human readers, or giving the judges exemplars, would also distinguish missing conventions from priors that the judges share through their training data. The candidate conventions came from inspecting the judges’ errors (Section [5.2](#S5.SS2 "5.2 Conventions the criterion texts leave unstated ‣ 5 Jev and the LLM Judges Err Alike ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")), and the cross-panel aggregations of Sections [5](#S5 "5 Jev and the LLM Judges Err Alike ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places") and [6](#S6 "6 Correlated Errors Undo the Cascade Advantage ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places") were computed after the offset had been observed. Because none of the four judges’ vendors discloses the training data, priors that the judges share cannot be ruled out.

#### Judge configuration.

The LLM judges are flash-tier models with the harness’s default prompt, used zero-shot, and Luna runs at its enforced temperature (Section [3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 The LLM judges ‣ 3 Judges, Panels, and Protocol ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). This matches the question of replacing the harness’s LLM judge with Jev but does not measure the best obtainable LLM judge. No larger model, few-shot prompt, wrapper for Jev on ordinal criteria, or LLM run without the prompt’s instructions on middle levels and self-contradiction was tried, and Jev Choice was not run without the NA option. The judges also receive different instructions and option orders (Sections [3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Jev ‣ 3 Judges, Panels, and Protocol ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places") and [3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 The LLM judges ‣ 3 Judges, Panels, and Protocol ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). We measured the LLM judges in both judging modes, but Jev only as designed: it receives a unit’s criteria together in a single request, and we did not test whether the questions of one request influence one another’s answers. Reasoning effort was requested only at medium (on all nine panels for DeepSeek, on the repeat units alone for Luna and Gemini) and at high, and only with per-criterion judgments; no LLM judge ran without reasoning or above high. Running the LLM judges without reasoning would narrow the cost ratios in either judging mode, with an unknown effect on accuracy.

#### Runs.

The main runs judged each ordinal panel once, and no judge is deterministic. Across five passes over RiceChem, about 3% of Jev’s pairs flipped verdict at least once, and in a spot check of 82 pairs from the ordinal panels two Luna runs gave the same verdict on 71 (Appendix [A](#A1 "Appendix A Panels, Judges, and Protocol ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). The repeat runs gave every LLM judge a second whole-rubric pass over 106 units (105 for DeepSeek), per-criterion judgments were repeated only in the effort calibration on the same units (Appendix [O](#A15 "Appendix O Reasoning Effort ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")) and in that spot check, and Jev was not rerun on an ordinal panel. The whole-rubric and effort runs followed the main runs by eight days under the same undated model names, so a model update or a change in routing could contribute to their differences from the main runs. DeepSeek’s medium and high runs overlapped in time, so we do not compare its wall times across effort levels, and because provider prompt caching and OpenRouter’s routing moved some costs, effort levels are compared on tokens and at fixed prices as well as on billed costs (Appendix [O](#A15 "Appendix O Reasoning Effort ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). OpenRouter routed DeepSeek’s whole-rubric run to a different mix of upstream providers than its main run, and its billed cost exceeded the harness’s estimate far more for its whole-rubric requests (Appendix [N](#A14 "Appendix N Whole-Rubric Judgments ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")), so routing, not only the judging mode, may lie behind DeepSeek’s smaller saving. On the ordinal panels the per-criterion runs also sent more requests at a time than the whole-rubric runs, so we do not compare wall times between judging modes.

#### Samples.

Results for single criteria rest on 25 to 258 units, because the ordinal panels are 10% or 20% samples. Two results rest on very little data. Jev Choice answered only 8 error_recovery pairs, and the \_nofact result comes from three contexts. The confident-error sample takes only 12 errors from each ordinal panel, whereas the band analysis of Figure [3](#S6.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 6.1 Jev’s confidence ranks its own errors ‣ 6 Correlated Errors Undo the Cascade Advantage ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")b uses every error.

#### Statistics.

The main intervals resample units, not the larger sampling groups, and Section [4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Accuracy in paired comparisons ‣ 4 Jev Can Often Replace an LLM Judge ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places") and Appendix [C](#A3 "Appendix C Paired Comparisons and Secondary Metrics ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places") report what changes when the groups are resampled instead. The 5-point equivalence margin is post hoc (Section [3.4](#S3.SS4 "3.4 Metrics and statistics ‣ 3 Judges, Panels, and Protocol ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")), and because it is absolute it is lenient where accuracy is low, as on ELLIPSE. The criteria singled out in the confidence analysis and the cut-offs of Appendix [G](#A7 "Appendix G The Offset in Detail ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places") were chosen after inspecting the data. The location analysis of Section [5.1](#S5.SS1 "5.1 A shared shortfall on ordinal criteria ‣ 5 Jev and the LLM Judges Err Alike ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places") is leave-one-out, but its one-level ELLIPSE shift and the USR shift of Section [5.2](#S5.SS2 "5.2 Conventions the criterion texts leave unstated ‣ 5 Jev and the LLM Judges Err Alike ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places") were chosen on the pairs they are scored on. Cascade gains under oracle thresholds are optimistic, the cross-fitted estimates rest on halves as small as 12 units, cascade costs are estimates under the rules of Appendix [K](#A11 "Appendix K Cascades ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places"), which do not charge for what a live cascade would also defer because Jev abstained, and only deferral on the confidence of Jev Choice or Jev Noul was tried; the LLM judges’ token probabilities or verbalized confidence were not elicited.

#### Labels and text.

Nearly half of the ELLIPSE labels depend on the rounding rule (Section [3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Panels and labels ‣ 3 Judges, Panels, and Protocol ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). Our rubric versions depart from the benchmarks’ own questions and rubrics (Appendix [A](#A1 "Appendix A Panels, Judges, and Protocol ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")), and we compared them with what the raters read for ELLIPSE alone. On the other six ordinal panels a convention that our texts omit may be stated in the raters’ own instructions. The HelpSteer2 and LFQA labels partly reflect something other than the named criterion. HelpSteer2 correctness tracks overall helpfulness at Pearson $r = 0.94$, and provenance alone predicts LFQA labels about as well as the judges do on exact accuracy. A judge may also have seen the items or labels of these public benchmarks in training.

#### Scope.

We tested one typed classifier. Later requests under the same model names may return different verdicts, because no request named a dated model version (Section [3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 The LLM judges ‣ 3 Judges, Panels, and Protocol ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places") and Appendix [A](#A1 "Appendix A Panels, Judges, and Protocol ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). The ordinal panels are all English, four of the seven are dialogue, and the longest ordinal unit is 1,114 words. Prices are list or billed prices on the dates of the runs, and the LLM judges’ cost depends on reasoning defaults that providers can change.

## Ethics Statement

This study uses public benchmarks as their authors released them and collects no new human-subject data. HealthBench’s health-related conversations serve only as evaluation material. We report every judge’s cost from recorded token usage and list or billed prices (Appendix [B](#A2 "Appendix B Cost and Wall Time ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places")). The authors are not incentivized by TypeSafe, which develops Jev, or by its industry competitors. We release the verdict records of every judge so that the paper’s numbers can be recomputed without any request to a model API, and Appendix [M](#A13 "Appendix M Release and Derived Statistics ‣ Jev vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places") lists the records and the values that need other inputs.
